: Hate Based on Fear 
[We hate chiefly because we 
think we are hated and we put up 
an instunt and savage defence. 
The next thing is offence. 

It is very bad in families. Chil- 
dren with a suspicious parent soon 
learn to think they are surround- 
ed by jealous enemies and that no 
one wishes them good. 

A most amazing thing happens 
when the erstwhile tormented onc 
ean, by sheer force of will, face 
about and start Nking people in- 
stead of fenring them. He, or she, 
will begin to enjoy” life ns they 
never did before, . ° 

You cannot love everybody, but 
one can develop a tolerance and 
indifference that goes far. There 
are even times for righteous indig- 


pration and protest, but such caacs 


are rare in comparison. They 
won't .eat the heart out and gen-. 
erate hate. Most hate Ia based

THE UNIVERSITY'S ° 
PROBLEMS 


Stress ig laid in the annual 
report of - the University of 
Hongkong on the point that if 
the demand for admission con- 
tinues to inerease, it can only be 
met by new buildings and addi- 
tional staff. The only alterna- 
tive is to refuse admission to 
those seeking to enter. It is, 
of course, a very gratifying cir- 
cumstance that’ the number of 
students at present enrolled ex- 
ceeds by more than sixty any 
previous enrolment, but, as ‘the 
Vice-Chancellor points out, this 
very fact throws the problem of 
the University’s future into 
relief, The whole issue is one 
of finance. Despite the fact 
that the University now — re- 
ceives an annual grant of $3650,- 
000 from the Government, as 
well as stibstantial--" 
terest from the Boxer In- 
demnity funds, ‘the past year 
reveals a deficit on working, 
mainly due to the fact that the 
yield from, investments has 
declined, that income from other 
sources has also shrunk, and 
that: the. new salaries scale has 
materially udded to the cost of 
administration, Any — marked 
expansion of the University’s 
uctivities in tha immediate 


future must, unfortunately, bo 


ruled out, since there. is small 
likelihood of any appeal for 
funds yielding substantial re- 
sults... Indeed, tthe last appeal 
put forward produced no res- 
ponse whatever. Nor can there 
be the Jenst prospect of any in- 
erease in the Goverriment grant, 
which now stands at a figure 
seven times that paid a few 
years back. The whole trouble 
—and it ‘is one with which the 
University has had to contend 
during the whole period of its 
existence—is that existing en- 
dowments are in. no sense adc- 
quate. One point which sug- 


| gests itself is whether, in view 


of prevailing conditions, stu- 
dents’ fees might not be in-’ 
creased. ‘At present, the umount 
received from this source is less 
than twenty per cent, of the 
yearly income It has been 
estimated that the annual ex- 
penses of a University student 
in Hongkong come to about 
$1,400, wherens it would cost a 
Chinese student going abrond 
for his University education at 
least $3,500. year, exclusive of 
‘travelling expenses, Whilst it 
might be unwise to take any 
step likely to affect the growing

AND LIVE FRUGALLY” 


. Estate in Hongkong to the value 
of $72,762 was left by Chan Chan- 


pong, aliag Chan A-tok, a house- | 


painter, who died, intestate, on 
Fane 2,'1924. Letters of ‘adminis- 
tration have been granted to Chan 
Yiu-kui, a son, on another gon and 


a concubine of the deceasod re- 


nouncing their righta. 


Before his death recently in the 
country, Wong Sun, alias Wong 
Yuk-shan, a retired restaurant 
keoper, 
Namhoi district, Province of 
Kwangtung, made a will enjoining 
his accond and fourth sons, to 
both of whom he left’ In equal 
shares the major part of a fortune 
of $81,900, to “deal with family 
affairs in.a harmonious way, 
working and living with diligence 
and frugality, and abstain from 
incurring displensure in dealings 
with others, - 3

granted to the two sons by the 
Hongkong Supreme Court.

DESCRIPTION OF 
SOCCER REFS, 


The football neason having con- 
cluded, referees are looking for- 
ward to four or five months’ rest 
from their arduous duties. -Pos- 
sibly they are the most misunder- 
stood but most forebearing charac- 
ters of the game, and annually are 
subject of good-humoured gibes at 
gatherings of sportsmen. 


Mr, W. J. Nicholls, the secretary 
of the’ Tunbridge Wells 
‘League, and on active referee, had 
something to say upon the subject 
aut a recent football club dinner. 


He sald he had heard referees 
described—not only by the spec- 
tators, but by those in the high: 
est of places—os “mean, mer- 
cenary, moaning, despicable and 

abject objects of misery.” 


“But after all,” he said, 
“poforces aro not all quite like 
that, and they have a right to rank 
among the greatest martyrs of our 
country. I am sure nd section of 
tho community can bo more as- 


* sured of an ultimate and complete- 


‘ly happy Valhalla than referecs, 
for undoubtedly thoy serve their 
- purpose on earth,” 


“The qualifications that yo to 
the making of a referee are numer- 
ous,” he continued. “One needed 


‘ tho patience of Job, the stamina of 


n horse, the.speed of a haro, tho 
skin of n rhinoccrous, the eyes of 
a hawk, the tact of a Cabinet 
Minister and tha humility of a 
stained-glass window.” 


Why did ho roferee? “Heing & 
he ‘found it’ a cheap 


track, no baseball diamend, and 
sveral tennis courts, the total 
covering, 137 mow of xround at 
Rue du Pere Froe, French Con-

Chinese anthorities for the site of 


Pioneer Field, holds a notable 
place in the history of Chinese 
athletics as it was there eight 

vars ayo.—that the Eighth Far! 

stern Olympic Games were held 
and «there again where many 
memorable international contests 
in various branches‘of sports have 
taken place.

| RESPONSIBILITY OF 


CHINA IN ASIA 


(Continued from Pago 8.) 


necordance with tho genius of her 
people. f 93% 

- Let me iustrate n little what 1 
mean in saying that,Chineso poll- 
‘tical philosophy has always been 
fundamentally democratic. Undor 
the monarchy, almost from timo 
{mmemor nl, although the emperor 
wae culled the “Son.of Heaven” 
and ruled by the “WIL of Icaven", 
nevertheless the conceyition of the 
Divine Sight of Kinga nover pre- 
vatled In Chinese political thought, 
The Emperor was to Interpret the 
will of Heaven through the will 
of the people. Thus tho Shang. 
Shu'says: “Heaven sees what the 
people fec., Heaven hears what 
the people hear.” - Again, “The 
people are the roots of the Stato. 
When the roots are secure, the 
State will have pence.” In other 
words, the Emperor was to rule In 
the interest of the governed and 
not in the intercat of himself or 
the governors, So long as he ob- 
served and practised this precept, 
ao. Jong would he be regarded as 
ruling by the will of Heaven. On 
the other hand, if on Emperor be- 
eame tyrannical and aclfish, and 
disregarded the welfare of the. 
people so that there came discon- 
tent, unrest, and suffering In the 
country as a result of his rule, 
then he would be considered to 
have exhausted his mandate from 
Heaven and to have acted against 
the will of Heaven. In such 8 case 
he was no longer considered the 
Son of Heaven or an Emperor, but 
a disendowed and fsoluted —in- 
dividual and the people’s common 
enemy, tigainst whom the lowest of 
hiy subjects might toke, the low 
into his own hands. 

These precepts and. innumerable 
others in our classics, have exer- 
cised a profound influence in our 
long national existence, and have 
heen responsible for the rise and 
all of dynasties in China. They 
have produced qualities and a 
flavour in the general life of China 
which explain why our goclal rela- 
tions are on the whole equitable 
and our intellectual life demo- 
The tenant-farmer {s no 
Jess a man in the presence of his 
Iandlord, the landlord no more A 
man in the presence of hia tenant, 
and there hag never existed in 
China any class barriers. Our 
history 14 replete with Instances 
where men of most humble origins 
rose to the highest positions in the 
State. . 

It ia very germane to present 
conditions in Asin—and in Europe 
jalso—to point out this remote 
background of a steadily main- 
tained democratic tradition in 
China, All the moves of demo- 
cratic yrovernment at the turn of 
the 17th and 18th centuries in 
England-against the absolutism of, 
the Stuarts, that whole training~ 
ground for ‘the modern English 
State, lay. implicit in Chinese poli- 
tical philosophy from the begin- 
nings of our recorded history. It 
ia n ferviee to Asia and to the 
whole world to re-emphasise that 
to-day. For China's persistence 
with her democratic tradition and 
its modern inotruments actually 
means more for. Asin of this 20th 
century than all. the posturings 
and paradings, the alarums and 

excursions, by which other in- 
fluences seck to make the Asiatic 
welkin ring,

Claire Trevor, startcd to work on‘ 


her prosont film contract almost bo- 
fore tho train that brought her to

eduecnted, nt 20 a veteran of Brond-

nrrived in the film capital, one even-

at the statlon os sho was descending 
the steps handed her'the script of her 
first picture with the information that 
she wouldn’t be expected Lo start work 
until 9 o'clock the following morning. 
She finished that one in timo to start 
n necond and the third one came the 
day after Number 2 was completed. 
The third was “Tho Mad Game"? and 
in her role opposite Spencer Tracy 
aho found hor first. real chance in a 
dramatle screen part, She played the 
role of n girl. reporter in the story 
built against « background of tho 
‘Snatch Racket,’ the underworld’s term 
for kidnapping. Other directora on 
the Fox lot saw parts of “The Mad 
Game" in the projection room and 
most of them wanted Miss Trevor 
in their next pletures. The “break” 
went to Director James Tingling, who 
now has the dainty blond actress in

“Gambling Ship” 


The one ‘place in ‘America where 
everything: goes and the authorities 
con't do a thing about it, furnished the 
backyround for “Gambling Ship’ the 
thrillipacked melodrama which Is 
shawing at the Alhambra Thentre. 
Tho film's faction In set | abonrd a 
gambling ship, as the title explnina— 
n flonting casino, moored just beyond 
the reach of. loca! and federal 
authorities at the three-mile limit. 
Here the wealthyecan Uc up thier own 
versels, climb aboard, and win or 
lose. fortunes at roulette, dice, black- 
jack, or any one of n dozen other 
ames which are run openly, with- 
out: any restraint but that of the 
underworld which operates them. 
Cary Grant, Benita Hume, Jack 
Lhkue, Glenda Farrell ond Roscoe 
Karns play the leading roles in 
“Gambling Ship.” Grant is tho cent- 
ral figure in the action—a bigshot 
enstern gambler who goes west for a 
vacntion, and, in Los Angeles, comes 
in contact with the operators of 2 
nautical casino,, When they offer him 
an ‘interest. in’ it, he refuses. But, 
when LaRue, an old-time enemy, 
operator of a rival ship, goes gunning 
for him, Grant changes his mind, 
‘The events that follow are rapid and 
thrilling, Miss Hume plays opposite 
Grant. Their romance runs through 
the picture,-reaching a climax In the 
most exciting part of the events 
aboard the ship. . zs

Ing at any of ity joints. His 
theory was that o hole was made 
in the holder by some external 
means. Even then if would not 
have caused ‘any damnge outside 
of ithe holder, hnd the gas not be-

merely have escaped, and the hold-

accident was started with a com- 
paratively small hold; which be-

The gas was only burning un the

gus entered the ‘houses and yot 
ignited. The gas in the holder 
before it burnt would be absolute-

getting burnt, baler 


Used As Target. 


His theory wos that the gas! 
holder had been used as a target, 
as things were found In it. Al- 
though he had not seen anyone 
throwing things at it, articles had 
been ‘found in the yard. 

Describing the construction of 
the holder, witness .safd that the 
holder was constructed of acheavy 
stee! framework, ucross which thin 
steel sheets, pne-tenth of an inch 
in thickness were fastened. 
These sheets merely acted ag en- 
velopes, and were not called upon 
to withstand pressure of more 
than’ about one-third of a pound 
per square inch, Any object flung 
against the side of the holder 
would open up one of these thin 
plates, particularly if it struck the 
holder near a joint. It was pos- 
sible the accident. may have been 
caused in that way.

to-day, Light Dues may also he

It is explained in the Objects ond 
Reasons that: : Z : 

1, The object of this Ordinance in 
to ennble the colonial light ducs 
prescribed. by the Order in Council 
set out In the Appendix to be coullect- 
ed in Hongkong. 

2.° The twelve Iighthouses. and 
the buoy mentioned jn the Schedule 
to tho Order in Couneil are admints- 
tered by the Board of Trade and have 
been malntalned by the Guneral 
Lighthouse Fund which derives its 
revenues mainly from Jight duces 
collected in Great Britain, Nohern 
Treland and ‘the Irish Free State.~ 
; 3. They have been maintained and 
improved, at considerable expense, in 
the interests of shipping of all nations 
‘deriving bencft from the lights, and 
it has become necessary to recan- 
sider. the. incidence.._of. thc. cost of 
the service. To this end His Majesty 
has been pleased to make an Order in 
Council on the !1°th December, 1131, 
under section 640 of the Merchant 
Shipping Act, 1894, providing for 
the levy of a due, of one penny per 
ton on all ships passing and deriving

VALUABLE ADDITIONS MADE 
DURING 13 


During 1988 the valuable collec: 
tion of books on China and things 
Chinese which had been collected by 
the Hnankow Club was purchased 
by the University for $25,000. 


This Information ia contained in 
the University of Hongkong Annual 
Report, which was released for 
publi¢ation this week. 


The Hankow Club Collection, adda 
the Report, {a x unique collection of 
vome 3,000 volumes and contains 
many books which are now vory 
tare and some that are practically. 

+ Unobtainable. : 


The number. of volumes, Govern: 
mont publications, paniphicts, etc. 

_ received into the Library up. to 
December 21, 1938, was 34,467. 
‘To this bas to be added the Morrl- 
son Collection of over 2,000 volumes. 
The total number of nccesaions to 
the Library for 1932 and 1939, in- 
cluding the purchase from the Han- 
_kow. Club, was 6,620. . - : 


Dr. Coedes presented the Library 
in 1933: with a sect of valunble 
Archwcological: Memolra on Indo- 
China, Theso memoirs are written 
in French. A large number of 
valunble publications have © been

. Carnegie Institute at. Washington,

the public who apply for this con- 
cexsion. Graduates of any Univer- 
sity are admitted as readers, but 
owing to luck of accommodation 
the line has to be drawn there. 


The Medical Library is housed 
quite: upart from the. 

‘Library of the University in the 
Pathology Building. This arrange- 
ment is in itself undesirable and 
expensive, and, this apart, the 
room in which the Library Js now 
necommodated is badly needed for 
the extension of the pathological 
museum, (The re-organisation and 
improvement of this museum 
was, one of the matters stressed by 
Sir Richard Needham). - 


‘The Fung. Pheg Shan ‘ Chinese 
Library now contains 4,092 sets 
of books containing 36,456 volumes

$2,242," Mr. Cheng Tse-Yoong of

versity—also presented during 1933

oe New York, June 13. 

Congressmen appear divided: in 
their opinions of the suggoation of

statement in the War Debt Note 
presonted to Britain, thnt pny- 
ments may bo made in Kind, 

Among the Democrats. Mr. 
William Rainey, Speaker of tha 
House of Representatives, charac- 
terlses Mr. Hull's proposal as fine 
and the best yet proposed; while 
Senator Borah, ‘for the Re- 
publicans, says that tho suggeation 
requires furthor study, but firat 
impressions revent no help for the 
American taxpayer. 

Though the suggestion is ex- 
tended specifically to Britaln, it is 
beHeved that it would be ap 
plicable to all dabtors. There- 
fore, according to the Washington 
correspondent of the Herald- 
Tribune, it may open the way for 
compromise ‘settlement, under 
which the ‘United States may 
Accept quantities of rubber, tin. 
radium and go forth. The idoa ia 
to accopt goods not competing 
with American’ manufactures, 

It “it pointed out that, in 
addition to large imports of tin 
and. rubber. the United Statca 
buys British manganese from 
Indian and chrome from Rhodesia, 
while Belgium possesses imported 
radiym from her deposits in 
Africa. . 

Mr. Caldwell, a member of the 
House of Representatives, -has 
introduced a resolytion authoris- 
ing the Committee to study tho 
poasibilities of War Debt pay- 
ments in tin.—Reuter.

in the concessions refused ‘to allow 
Chinese officers to Inapect ‘and con: | 
trol the working conditions In thd’ 
factories. This was 8 ‘great 
anomaly, becnuss the concessions 
were on Chincac territory. 

Mr. Lie mentioned the Govern- 
mant’s efforts to cope with unem- 
ployment, ~by ° read building 
schemes, and particularly tho 
Three Year Plan. . . 

Also, he sald, the Government 
contempleted the development of 
industrial enterprises, including a 
Four Year Pian to cost $26,000,- 
000, and mentioned that four large 
works Rave already been establish- 
ed.— Reuter, 
a —SS 

plying official American ‘recogni- 
tion of the transfer difilculties; 
and, secondly, as offering prospects 
of.a solution by way of the barter 
of possibly tin, rubber, manganese 
and whisky. si 

It is pointed out that auch o 
transaction may cause serious dis- 
turbances of foreign exchanges, re- 
sulting in: Firstly, further 
strengthening: of the dollar, which 
the American authorities do not 
desire; and,: secondly, the upsct- 
ting of the equilibrium of Empire 
currencies, o8 Britain would have 
to. buy the tin and other commo- 
dities from the Emplre. 

:Others are of tho opinion ‘that 
payment fn kind will not appenl to 
the British Government owing to 
the aforementioned difficulties and

the inter-dependence of all Inter- 
j Sovernmental War Debts.—Reuter.

“MILITARY "FUNERAL, | 


LAST HONOURS RENDERED 
TO PTE, A, MORRIS - 


A military funeral cortege 
entoring the Protestant Cometory 
yesterday afternoon, marked tho 
passing of 2 British soldier, 
Private Archibald Morris. of | the 
lat Battallon South | Wales 


Borderers, whose trayle death by |] 


drowning occurred on Tuesday at 
the Stonecutters Services’ bathing 
bench. 


A native of Bristol, Pte. Morris 
was only 24'years of age. He join- 
ed -the regiment moro than five 
yenrs ago, and of this perlod two- 
and-half yeara wore spent with the 
1st Battalion in service abroad. 


Ho was unmarried. His parents 
are Hving near Potypridd. 


Full, military honours wore 
rendered ut: the funeral . Tho. 
cortege, with the coffin draped in a 
Union Jack and drawn on a gun 
carriage, wis headed: by a firing 
party and. Band: With a full 
muster of “C,” tho deceased’s 
company, were detachments from 
the ‘Lincolns and Enat Lances., the 
whole being under the command 
of Captain C. F. Blackden, of “C” 
Company, Ist. Batt. Bouth Wal 
Borderers. Other officers‘ present 
wero the Officer Commanding the 
1st Batt. .tho South Wales 
Borderers, Major P. Gottwaltz, 
M.C., and Lt..D, Peel Yates and 2nd 
Lt. RUC. H. Miera, of “C” Com- 
pany. : an 


Tho service at the graveside waa 
read by the Rev. J. N, Lewis Bryan, 
Three volleys were fired over the 
grave, followed by the sounding of 
the “Last Post’ and “Reveille.” 

Wreaths were sent by His Ex- 
cellency the General Officer Com- 
manding the Troops in Hongkong 
and South Chinn (Major-General 
O. C. Borrett) ; the Battalion Com- 
mander, Major P, Gottwaltz, uc, 
other military units of the 
Garrison, and from members ‘of 
Ist Batt, South Wales Borderers,

000,000 on attaining his majority, 


broke off his engagement to Miss

parents. 


HONEYMOON OFFER! _ 
“tf her parents had not inter 
fered everything probably would

Pittsburgh, June’13. 

A great show-down in the 

increasing labour unrest in 


the United States may be 
forthcoming shortly. 


Matters are likely to ba 
brought to a head if the 
crisis threatening in the 
steel industry -develops. 
~—Phete ave suniours ‘ot plans for 
repressive measures that are, it is 
suid, now ‘being developed. in 
Washington. - 

With the Administration and the 
country waiting ‘tensely, hoping 


against hope that something will 
be accomplished at the cleventh- 


hour' to ward off the strike of]. 


400,000 steel ‘workers, delegates 
from stecl mills all over the 
country are pouring into Pitts- 
burgh by car, bus and train for 
the Convention of the Amalgamat- 
ed Association of: Iron, Steel and 
Tin Workers, at which the strike 
devision will be trken. 


READY FOR STRIKE. 


.The leaders of the Union claim 
that over one hundred. thousand 
workers out of 400,000 had declared 
their readiness to down tools at any 
moment. a 


They assert that-.they ure able! Exercise of the Central 


to close the activities of nearly 
every mill in the country. 

More conservative leaders, like 
Mr. Tighe, are striving to rench 4 


J vompromise to prevent the strike. 


The principal demand of — the 
steel workers is recognition of 
their right to choose their own 
representatives for -. collective 
bargaining, ‘in other . words, the 
termination of the system of Com- 
pany Unions in the atecl industry. 


PRESIDENT SEEKING POWERS. 


A report from Washington say 
that sweeping powers to enable 
President Roosevelt to deal with 
the strike are reported to be in 
preparation and ‘will be‘contained 
in n new Labour Resolution, the 
draft of which it is stated js. be- 
Ing rushed by the Administration. 
—Reuter. et

TERRORISM IN 
AUSTRIA © 


NAZI INVADERS 
ACCUSED 


‘ Vienna, June 13. 
The Austrian Government, 
despite the pressure of the 
Heimwehr leaders, is still refrain- 
ing. from violéntly, forceful mea- 
sures against the terrorist acti- 
vities. 

In an endeavour to check the 
epidemic of terrorist outrages, the 
Director of Security in. the 
Province of Voralberg has now 
ordered the complete closing of 
the German frontler, , 

This is as far ‘as the authorities 
are at present prepared to xo. 

Even minor traffic is prohibited, 
The peasants along the frontier 
are to be prevented from crossing 
and re-crossing 48 is their wont. 


GERMANS ACCUSED. 


-The order declares that the 
frontior_.will remain. closed_until 
the outrages cease and alleges that 
Germaine have ‘been crossing the 
‘frontier every night into Voralberg 
in order to commit outrages. 

The Austrian Government has 
submitted to the Italian Under- 
Secretary. for Foreign Affairs, 
Signor Suvich, all the material 
that hag been ‘collected which 
could prove that Germany is guilty 
of inspiring the outbreaks of 
violence, 

SLACKENING EXPECTED. © 


The Government, however, ¢x- 
pects a slackening of the tension 
and has not, therefore, taken the 
ngyregsive steps demanded by the 
Heimwehr. 

Dr. Dolifuss probably. has high 
hopes ‘that the Hitler-Mussolini, 
conversations at Stra ‘will bring an 
end to Germany's active. stimula- 
tion of ‘chaos -in Austria against 
Italian interests.— Reuter.

The joint paid circulations of the South China Morning Post 
and The Hongkong Telegraph average approximately— 


9,000 COPIES PER DAY | 


’ This means that your advertising message enters nine thou- 
sand homes and is read by probably three times that number 
of people daily. 

If you endeavour to reach these readers by any other means, 
such as circular letters, how much extra does it cost you? 


_ Here is a simple calculation based on a reasonable estimate of © 
the work. involved:— 


9,000 Circular letters ........... @ $7-per thou: $ 63.00 
9,000 Envelopes ......... scl eas » oo» $ 36,00 
9,000 Postage Stamps ...........@ 2 cents each $180.00 . 
Labour and incidental expenses (approx:) ....... $ 21.00 

. - ‘Total... veces. $300.00 
AN ADVERTISEMENT OCCUPYING 634” by 10” (approxi- 


mately the size ofa circular letter) published in both the 
S. C. M. Post and The Hongkong Telegraph— 


COSTS. ONLY $36.00. 


_ (Based on an Annual Contract. of 500” @ 60 cents), 
You know what happens to circulars and you know what 
_ “happens to newspapers when they enter the home or office. 
.-—“The former may fall into the waste-paper basket. THE 
"LATTER ARE ALWAYS READ! oe? 
‘In other . words, by placing. your. message before the 
newspaper-reading public, your $300 will give you nearly

FOOCHOW. NOTES 


REDS STILL YERY 


“ACTIVE, |. 
. Foochow, June 10.” 
Up ton fow. days ago, the 


farmera were yreatly distressed 
by the serioua risk of drought, 98 
there had been no rain worth men- 
tioning for many weeks. But now 
everything is changed, and after a 
few thunder-storms as a kind of 
warning, ‘2 stendy downpour has 
set in, With the usual result that 
people are now snylng there is too 
much. Low-lying ground is al- 
rendy under water, and it leoka as 
if the rice plants which were so 
recently dying of thirst, will soon 
be destroyed by the flood. 

The process of restoring order 
and security throwrhout the Pro- 
vince goes | on very slowly. In 
West Fukien, where the Reds are 
supposed to be surrounded 
(“blockaded” is the official term) 
by the Central Army troops, they 
still appear to have a large amount 
of Hberty, und quite recently they 
were able to occupy the City of 
Yungan after heavy fighting. 
They withdrew in a few days with 
plenty of loot and many captives: 
but # large body of “Red Uandits” 
came in and did much damage: 

On May 9, Lu Hsing-yung at- 
tacked the City and drove out the 
bandits; but much of the loss 
(especially that of electric-light 
plant) cannot be made good .for 0 
Jong. time. More recently Shahsien 
has heen-taken and evacuated by 
the Reds, and disturbances of 
yarying gravity are reported from 
almost every district. 

The | Government 
seem to ret great hopes on the 
opening of motor roads, which 
they are pressing forward as fast 
‘agthey can>ag-‘n-means of-keeping 
order, But the whole question is 
most complicated, and there seems 
to be no short-cut to the reign of 
peace which is so sorely needed.— 
Our Own Correspondent.

Awaiting agreement from Lon- 
on, the Duke of Gloucester will 
ve piloted by Sir Charles Kings- 
ford Smith In a series of flights 
to important ~coyntry centres in 
New South Wales during his visit 
to Australia in connection with 
the Centenary Celebrations. 


, , 

This will enable him to cover 
great distances during his nino 
days’ tour of the State.. 


Among notable Sydney fixtures, 
tells Austral News, fs a Garden 
week when the Duke will be asked 
to plant a tree in the private 
gurden judged best by the com- 
mittee. eS 


Victoria's latest plans for wel- 
coming the Royal visitor include 
the sending of a combined yacht- 
Ing flect to greet the Duke when 
he arrives in Port Phillip Bay. 
Interest,in the ten-day Regatta, 
from December 26th.to January 
8th, is growing apace and final 
arrangements have been made for 
the trans-Tasman (Australia to 
New Zealand) ,yacht race for 
which a £60 cup has been’ given by 
the Auckland Yachting Club. 


GOODWILL MISSION. 


AUSTRALIA: BENEFITS BY FAR 
. EASTERN TOUR 


Brisbane, June 18." 

‘The Hon. Mr. John G, Latham, 
who has just returned to Australla 
from the Far East, when inter- 
viewed to-day said that he had 
found in the Far East a serlous 
misunderstanding: about Austra- 
lia, which had only been removed 
by frank statements. ; 


The’ Mission would result In 
vast benefita for Australia in her 
relations with the countries of 
the’, Far East, Mr. Latham be- 
Nieved. 

Further, the Mission has learn- 
ed much of the greatest valuc to 
Australia on the tour, he said. 

One of the results of the 
Missiott would be that in all 
probability Australin would ap- 
point three Trade Commissioners 
tu Far Eastern: countrics, 

_Mr. Latham refused to comment 
on Japanese influence in the

FUTURE POLICY. 


Whether we are ever accorded 
fiscal praference, alongside all 
other British brewed beers, de- 
pends on the future policy of the 
Colony. We are a free port ac- 
cording, for the most part, equality 
of opportunity to all nations. 
Our ideal position and our har- 
bour facilities have in’ the pust 
fully justified our policy of re- 
maining a free port, but naw that 
our free port is surrounded by the 
barrier reefs of high tarttts and 
our great entrepot tradevix dwind- 
fing, it is at least questipnable 
whether ‘it is a policy we should 
rigidly adhere to,

In seconding the ‘adoption of the

eorlicat stages. :

rasament by the obvious and pre- 
fituble menns of expanding our

SIR WM. SHENTON'S VIEW. 


Sir William Shenton, a Director 
of the Company, in the course of 
au brief speech, remarked:—On the 
question raised by our Chairman, 
as.to a protective tariff in this 
Colony for our beer, 1 -fear on 
general principles, [am not at 
the moment able to subscribe to 
all he says, The prosperity of 
this Colony has, over a period of 
nearly a hundred years, been built 
up upon tho basis of what is 
known aa the free port. principle, 
and T, for one, am not prepared 
to go back on this’ policy without 
very’ deliberate and careful con- 
sideration. In these days, tariffs, 
quotas, and trade agreements, are 
common topics, A change in this 
direction in’ this Colony, in my 
opinion, is largely a question for 
our .bankers, merchants, shippers, 
insurance companies, manufactur- 
erg, etc., to, decide, and until they 
have given us gtheir considered 
opinions, I .desire to reserve my 
judgment. : :

‘DEBATE. IN’ THE 
COMMONS 


_ London, June 13. 
‘The Report of the Com- 
mittee of Privileges on the 
Hoare-Derby case is beg 
debated in the House of 
Commons ' to-night. : ; 


The debate was opened by the 
Prime, Minister who, In ‘a 
briof xpedch, —_moved that 
the House agree with the report, 
and traced,the circumstances which 
led to the questions raised by Mr. 
‘Winston Churchill being referred 
to the Committers. 

He called attention to the 
paragraph tn the report stating that 
“Your Committee unanimously re- 
port that no brench of privilege has 
been committed by Sir Samuel | 2 
Hoare or by the Harl of Derby.” 

His duty was to put the report 
before House. 

Tt was due to Sit Samuel Hoare 
and Lord Derby that the Ipouse 
should give a clear-cut decision as 
to whether or not they were Kauilty 
of a breach of privilege. 

Mr. Winston Chorchill, speaking 
immediately after Mr. Ramsay 
MacDonald, anid he did not modify 
his original charxe, and claimed 
that the report established that the 
Secretary of State for Indian and 
Lord Derby, jointly and severally, 
from the best of motives and in 
n perfeetly honourable ‘manner, en- 
denvoured to deter the Manchester 
Chamber of Commerce from pre- 
senting the evidence that they de- 
sired before the joint committee 
on Indian constitutional reforma,-.- 
British Wirelesk.

a + Aepest scearee by forged orders made out in

came before Mr. Macfadyen in

morning.

pleaded guilty te no fewer than 
seven counts of obtaining liquors 
and groceries from the Wing On 
Company, The Sun’ Company, 
|Sincere Company and Lane Craw- 
ford, by means of forged order 
notea. ‘The total value of the 
goods obtained was $47.

Inspector J, Murphy that defendant 
was formerly employed 1s cook by 
Mrs, Cardwell, No, 520 The Peak, 
but he was dismissed for drinking. 
Subsequent to, his being sacked,

naling ‘Tau Poi-chal, a atrect coolie,

Washington, June 13, 

The Treasury Department has 
revealed that a Filipino arrested 
in Kowloon confessed to counter- 
feiting ten-dollar U, S. notes to 
the value of U.S:$15,000 

Thirty-nine of the notes, it was 
stated by the fecused, were turned 
over to nn agent in Shanghad, 

His confession impliented four 
other Filipinos and a Peruytan 
Chinese. —United Presa, 

The case wan brought’ to on 
head quite recently, following In- 
formation that American $10 notes 
were belng forged somewhere in 
Chinese territory,

The body of Mr, E. R. Collier, 
the Chinese Maritimes Customs 
boat officer, who met his death 
under tragic circumstances at Hoi- 
how a few, days "fo, fa being 
brought to Hongkong . for in- 
termont. It will be landed ‘at 
Queon's Pier at 2 p.m. to-morrow, 
and the funeral will pass the

stances in which Mr. Collier-met 
his death have .been received 
locally, but a brief message re- 
ceived from Hoihow. last evening 
stated that he succumbed in the 
American Hospital there .as 2 
regult of injures in an explosion.

Currency. 
In Britain 


MR, RUNCIMAN’S 
CLAIMS 


London; June 13.. 
‘The stability of managed 
currency and its advantages 
over a rigid gold standard 
were ‘stressed by the Presi- 


{dent of the Board of Trade 
Jin a public address to-day. 


Mr, Runciman, spenking at the 
annual luncheon of the Chambers 
of ‘Commerce of the British 
Empire, whose conference opencd 
In London to-day, said that so far 
na monetary policy was concerned: 
In tho United Kingdom, they 
enjoyed a sterling system that way 
the most stuble in the world. 

The old gold standard, to which 
they had been wedged, was’ no 
longer part and parcel of our 
economy, The sterling basla had 
praved steadier than they had 
antleipnated—steadier even than 

old. 

B Referring to the Ottawa agree- 
ments, he’ asserted tho view that 
their resulta were disnppointing, 

If circumstances had been more 
favourable they might have. done 
better, but he felt confident that, 
ns time went on, and relations with 
the Dominions were drawn closer, 
the tendency of the Ottawa ngrec- 
menty would be to add to the 
svalume of imoerial trade, —Rritinh,

FREE DIVIDENDS. 


NOVEL SHARE ISSUE SCHEME, 


BY LOCAL FIRM 


A stafl co-operative mecting of 
Messrs. . Bernards,’ of, Harwich, 
waa held yesterday afternoon; when 
the year’s trading activities were 
reviewed by the Managing Director 
for tha Far East. 


In the course of his . remarks, 


the Managing Director urged. th 
every opportunity should be taken 
to afford employment to British 
ermftamon and salesmen, and 
especialy at this time, when 
business was forging so Steadily 
ahend. This, he sald, could be 
easily accomplished by continuing 
that co-operation which had hither- 
to been so happily brought about 
by the retafling advantages the 
firm were able to proffer to their 
customers. and by the enthusiasm 
4o evident with ench member of 
the-staff, ; to 


There was ‘no doubt, the Mana- 
ging Director added, that every 
British man and woman carnestly 
desired to buy British goods, and 
thore was every reason to ap- 
precinte that the staffs were 
awnre that correct presentation 
waa the only next atep to complete 
satisfaction, 


He waa happy to mention that 
they would shortly be opening a 
block of seven shops simultancous- 
ly In one British town, This waa 
without precedent in the trade. 
In Hongkong, Bernards of Harwich 
were shortly moving into larger 
premises, which would permit the 
retailing of more extensive stocks 
and fuller scope for display and ser. 
vice. It was hoped that these pre- 
mises would be open by July 7. 


Customer Co-Operation. 


After detailing the activities of 
Bernards of Harwich throughout 
the world, the. Managing Director 
mentioned that: the business was 
now giving employment, directly 
and indirectly, to three thousand 
British ¢émployees, each one of 
whom, he stressed, reed upon the 
co-operation of British people re- 
sident abroad and to: the initiative 
of cach, member of the staffs for 
permanent employment, 


In furtherance of this it had been 
decided to invite customers to closer 
co-operation by the issie of shares 
as a complimentary — exprekslon of 
the existent relationship. Such 
shares would be in the denomina- 
tion of one dollar each and would 
be presented in blocks up to fifty. 
Yearly dividends would be payable 
with a minfmum of five per cent. 
per share!’ Printed — invitations 
would shortly be posted to cach 
customer, and it was hoped that 
this interesting innovation would 
lead to n renewed interest in Bri- 
tish enterprise overseas, -

HEAVY FINES ON 
TWO WOMEN 


In a case which came before 
Mr. Hamilton ‘this morning it 
was disclosed that a woman had. 
resold a.child for 8 profit of 80 
cents. ‘ ‘ 

“This is a very serious thing, 
trafitcking in children,” remarked 
Mr. Hamilton, when two marricd 
women, Wong Sai and Sit Yung, 
were charged with having taken 
part in 4 transaction tha object 
of which was to transfer posses- 


sion of a one-month-old ‘female}’ 


child for a valuable conatderatidn. 
Both women admitted — the 
charge. Wong Saf was fined 


$260, or three months’ imprison:|. 


ment,-and Sit Yung, who was the 
principal in the trananction, $500, 
or six months’ imprisonment. 
- -Suh-Inspector H.W. Fraser, of 
the S.C.A., said that on June 11 
the mother of the child reported 
to the No. 2 Police Station that 
the had presented her child to 
Sit Yung on June 10, und that 
she had received information 
that Sit Yung had resold the child 
to another woman. 
THE TRANSACTION. . 
Before the birth of the child, 
the mother borrowed $5 from Sit 
Yung to defray expenses of her 
confinement. After the child was 
born, Sit -Yung demanded tf” 
money back, and, when the mother 
said.she could not pay, demanded 
the child. The mother according- 
ly gave her the child, and Sit 
Yung paid an extra $2 to keep 
the child. 
. It was alleged that when the 
deed of presentation was drawn 
up by Sit Yung, she had given tho

NEW ERA. . 
IN CHINA’S | 
FINANCE — 


BUDGET ESTIMATE 
, MILESTONE 


-” CENTRALISING 
PROGRAMME 


Nanking, June 14.) | 


A’ great improvement in 
China's finance administration 
was claimed by a spokesman of 
the Ministry of Finance, in an 
interview with Reuter — this 
morning. - : 


He stated that the Budget pub- 
Mshed for. the fiscal year from July 
1, 1934, to June 80, 1935, was. the 
firat time that the Mibletry of 
Finance had. made puble an official 
estimnte of his anticipated revenucs 
and expenditures: ‘for, the coming 
year. a 


"Such action was a demonstration 
that the country’s finances are be- 
Ing managed according to a defined 
plan, due to the degree of stabilian- 
tion and order which had now been 
established by the Central Govern- 


ment of China, and -to the steady, 


efforts which have been. made to 
centralisc-and organise the coun- 
try’s financial system along modern 
Nines,.—Renter. 


e

Tsang Hong, unemployed, was pro- 
duced hefore Mr; Hamilton, at the 
Central Magistracy this morning, and 
charged with trespassing, and being 
in possession of an {natrument for an 
unlawful purpose, He waa fined 
$500, or six months’ hard labour on 
the first charge, and $260, or ‘threo 
months, on the second, the sentences 
to run consecutively, : :

"UNIVERSITY LIBRARIES 


VALUARLE ADDITIONS MADE 
DURING 1933 


During 1933 the valuable collec- 
flon of books on China and things 
Chinese which had been collected by 
the Hankow Club was purchased
